TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* A Bumpy Peace Election in the Philippines
  Foreign Policy | 16 Sep 05:00 UTC
  On a majority-Muslim island, federalists are sparring with Islamists.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: An election in the Philippines’ Bangsamoro Autonomous Region was supposed to settle a decades-old conflict ; Singapore defends high ministerial salaries ; and app drivers in Vietnam assert their growing political importance with a strike . Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: An election in the Philippines’ Bangsamoro Autonomous Region was supposed to settle a decades-old conflict ; Singapore defends high ministerial salaries ; and app drivers in Vietnam assert their growing political importance with a strike . This week in the southern Filipino archipelago of Mindanao , an election in the Bangsamoro Autonomous Region was supposed to settle a decades-old conflict. In Cotabato City, one of the region’s larger cities, I served as an election observer, accompanying a team from the International Republican Institute. Unfortunately, things were bumpy. And whether this election is the next step toward peace or the starting gun for more conflict is yet to be determined. At least five people were killed and 18 injured over the election period, despite the heavy presence of security forces. Decades of conflict and an internal split between rebels evidently amplified tensions. First, though, some context. Mindanao , the Philippines’ second-largest island, has a long history of struggle against national authorities. Spanish and then American colonialists struggled to subdue the majority-Muslim islands. In the 1960s under President Ferdinand Marcos, an influx of Christian settlers from other islands sparked another round of conflict. In 1996, a rebel group called the Moro National Liberation Front (MNLF) struck a peace deal with the government that held despite imperfect implementation. The Moro Islamic Liberation Front (MILF), an Islamist splinter from the MNLF, kept fighting until it struck a deal in 2014. On the political track, the agreement had two key components. First, the establishment of the Bangsamoro Autonomous Region in Muslim Mindanao (BARMM), an autonomous parliamentary government straddling numerous Muslim-majority areas in Mindanao with wide-ranging powers to raise revenue and legislate. Second was an election, to choose delegates for the parliament to replace its initial appointees. The politics have been tricky, though. After BARMM formed in 2019, its first election was initially scheduled for 2022 but then subsequently delayed four ti

* Trump and Xi Won’t Find Common Ground on AI Safety
  Foreign Policy | 15 Sep 21:35 UTC
  The leaders have fundamentally different ideas about the technology and how to regulate it.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: Prospects for AI safety measures appear dim ahead of the U.S.-China summit, Chinese President Xi Jinping attends the BRICS summit in India, and China moves to replenish its oil stockpiles . Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: Prospects for AI safety measures appear dim ahead of the U.S.-China summit, Chinese President Xi Jinping attends the BRICS summit in India, and China moves to replenish its oil stockpiles . U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping are scheduled to meet in Washington on Sept. 24, and artificial intelligence safety is likely high on the agenda. Many analysts hope that China and the United States might cooperate to set basic international principles for governing the technology despite their geopolitical rivalry—much as the Soviet Union and United States ultimately cooperated during the Cold War on nuclear arms control, polio eradication , and protecting the ozone layer . China has reportedly threatened to cancel the summit if the United States goes ahead with arms sales to Taiwan beforehand, but that sounds like routine diplomatic noise to me. Even if the meeting goes ahead, the chances of productive alignment on AI safety are low. As I wrote last month, the two countries have fundamentally different understandings of AI risk. While the U.S. tech elite is preoccupied with sci-fi scenarios involving singularity and machine gods , Chinese bureaucrats operate within a tradition of cybernetics that is more concerned with technical failures and the political and economic consequences of new technologies. Simply put, if you think the central AI problem is “control the emergence of superintelligence,” your approach will differ from someone whose concern is “prevent AI from worsening youth unemployment.” In theory, there are still shared problems that Beijing and Washington could address at the summit, particularly in the wake of the OpenAI-Hugging Face security breach . As much as the Chinese internet is cut off from the wider world, it still depends on many of the same systems and crumbling infrastructure as the United States. Still, another issue is that the United States and China have mismatched regulatory capacities, making it unlikely that they could impose shared standards. For better or worse, China exercises tight control over its tech sector. That has included massive fines, canceled IPOs , ideological restrictions,
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/15/china-us-ai-safety-trump-xi-summit-diplomacy/

The U.S. agricultural sector has been in an uproar over Trump’s recently announced plan to boost imports of ground beef at a reduced tariff rate, part of a broader effort to drag down record beef prices. The U.S. agricultural sector has been in an uproar over Trump’s recently announced plan to boost imports of ground beef at a reduced tariff rate, part of a broader effort to drag down record beef prices. Ground beef prices surged yet again in August, powered by tight supplies and continuously strong demand for the staple meat. The plan, which was announced last month, would see 300,000 metric tons of lean beef trimmings enter the country for 90 days. It comes as the sector reels from surging energy and fertilizer prices, ongoing trade whiplash, and sweeping cuts to the U.S. Department of Agriculture. The president of the American Farm Bureau Federation, the country’s largest agricultural lobby, blasted the move as a “gut punch to ranchers’ bottom line.” Retiring Sen. Thom Tillis also heaped on the criticism. “If you think that providing subsidized beef for some period of time is going to make farmers happy and prices go down on a systemic basis, you’re wrong. Doesn’t happen,” he told reporters last month. As midterms loom, that backlash could prove consequential. “We’ve got 71 days” until the election, Tillis added at the time, “and right now we don’t have a positive message to tell farm country.” “The farming community has proved it’s willing to tolerate a fair amount of indignity and injury, but there’s only so much they can take before they revolt,” Christopher Barrett, an agricultural economist at Cornell University, told Foreign Policy . * Are Chinese Firms Handing the United States Data?

  AI competition may be an unwitting weakness for Beijing.
  FULL TEXT: A recent report by the U.S. artificial intelligence company Anthropic accused several leading Chinese AI firms, including Moonshot AI and DeepSeek, of distilling Claude’s capabilities on a large scale. Anthropic said some of these companies went beyond using Claude to generate training data and, without users’ knowledge, in one instance routed at least 300,000 requests from real users through the model over a 10-day period. The data reportedly sent to Claude included surveillance material submitted by a user Anthropic suspected of being affiliated with the People’s Liberation Army; internal code and valid credentials from engineers at major Chinese state-owned enterprises; information from police case management systems; and details of internal AI projects at Chinese technology companies. A recent report by the U.S. artificial intelligence company Anthropic accused several leading Chinese AI firms, including Moonshot AI and DeepSeek, of distilling Claude’s capabilities on a large scale. Anthropic said some of these companies went beyond using Claude to generate training data and, without users’ knowledge, in one instance routed at least 300,000 requests from real users through the model over a 10-day period. The data reportedly sent to Claude included surveillance material submitted by a user Anthropic suspected of being affiliated with the People’s Liberation Army; internal code and valid credentials from engineers at major Chinese state-owned enterprises; information from police case management systems; and details of internal AI projects at Chinese technology companies. The leakage of sensitive information will of course alarm Beijing. But compared with one or two pieces of sensitive material reaching the United States, the bigger worry may be that, in the process of distilling the capabilities of the most advanced U.S. models, Chinese AI companies may also be sending large quantities of real Chinese user data to the United States. AI competition is not just about compute but about data. Today, data available on the open internet is increasingly easy to obtain. What is truly scarce is the high-quality data generated by real tasks: why users turn to AI, what problems they are trying to solve, what background materials they provide, how far they have progressed in writing code, what technical obstacles companies encounter, and what they want AI to help them accomplish. Such data can be far more valuable than the ordinary detritus of the web. On its own, s
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/15/china-us-ai-artificial-intelligence-data-summit-anthropic/

* Brazil’s Election Won’t Fix Its Biggest Problems
  Foreign Policy | 15 Sep 14:43 UTC
  Slow growth and corruption have dogged the right and the left.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. On Oct. 4, Brazilians will vote in the first round of a presidential election with two clear front-runners who appear to be opposites. Incumbent President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva is running on a platform of economic populism, social liberalism, and political stability. His main challenger, Sen. Flávio Bolsonaro , is campaigning as the torchbearer of the socially conservative movement that his now-convicted father , President Jair Bolsonaro, launched in 2018. Despite these competing visions, both candidates are unlikely to solve Brazil’s core issues: anemic long-term growth and entrenched corruption. Hastening growth requires structurally lower interest rates, which means deep fiscal reform. Addressing corruption and impunity, meanwhile, would require a wholesale reappraisal of Brazil’s post-dictatorship justice system. On Oct. 4, Brazilians will vote in the first round of a presidential election with two clear front-runners who appear to be opposites. Incumbent President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva is running on a platform of economic populism, social liberalism, and political stability. His main challenger, Sen. Flávio Bolsonaro , is campaigning as the torchbearer of the socially conservative movement that his now-convicted father , President Jair Bolsonaro, launched in 2018. Despite these competing visions, both candidates are unlikely to solve Brazil’s core issues: anemic long-term growth and entrenched corruption. Hastening growth requires structurally lower interest rates, which means deep fiscal reform. Addressing corruption and impunity, meanwhile, would require a wholesale reappraisal of Brazil’s post-dictatorship justice system. Brazil’s problem is not an inability to reform. It is the country’s inability to institutionalize those changes into lasting solutions. The country has passed legislation attempting to address slow growth and corruption multiple times since redemocratization in the 1980s, but in most cases, politicians either later watered down those reforms or rolled them back entirely. Brazil is trapped in a cycle of crisis, reform, and backsliding. Breaking out of this cycle is possible, but only if the country can overcome the fragmented political landscape that makes it difficult for voters to attribute policy outcomes to the politicians responsible for them.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/15/brazil-election-lula-flavio-bolsonaro-corruption-economy-debt/

--- Energy (5) ---

* CCC: Heathrow expansion could push flights to ‘80% of UK emissions by 2050’
  Carbon Brief | 15 Sep 23:01 UTC
  Aviation is on track to be responsible for 80% of the UK’s carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions by 2050, according to… The post CCC: Heathrow expansion could push flights to ‘80% of UK emissions by 2050’ appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Aviation is on track to be responsible for 80% of the UK’s carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions by 2050, according to the Climate Change Committee (CCC). Emissions from flying have more than doubled since 1990 – driven by rising passenger numbers – even as the climate impact of every other sector in the UK economy has fallen . The UK does not have “credible” policies in place to reverse this trend of rising emissions, says the CCC in new advice to the government on future aviation policy. The government has signalled its support for expanding Heathrow, the nation’s largest airport, while relying on “ techno-fixes ” such as “ sustainable aviation fuels ” (SAFs) to cut emissions. Yet, even without Heathrow expansion, the CCC says aviation emissions are on track to be higher in 2050 than they are today – reaching 38m tonnes of CO2 (MtCO2). As the chart below shows, this would account for most of the remaining CO2 from the UK economy, all of which would need to be removed from the atmosphere in order to meet the legal target of net-zero emissions. Expanding Heathrow would add another 2.4MtCO2 in 2050, amounting to around 5% of all the UK’s emissions. (This would increase to 4.5MtCO2 when expansion is complete in 2054.) With a final decision on Heathrow expansion expected by 2029, the government asked the CCC for its advice on whether the plan is compatible with the UK’s climate targets. The CCC has concluded that the UK simply lacks sufficient policies to reduce aviation emissions and “expanding Heathrow would compound the problem”. In a press briefing, CCC chair Nigel Topping told journalists: “The UK does not currently have a credible plan to reduce [aviation emissions] in line with net-zero, so that creates a serious challenge for meeting our climate commitments.” The “ jet-zero strategy ”, launched by the previous Conservative government in 2022, set out plans to cut aviation emissions. However, the Labour government has since accepted that the strategy’s expectations for SAFs, electric planes and fuel-efficiency improvements were unrealistic. The CCC says a “credible and robust net-zero policy framework for aviation” should be set out in a revised strategy, which is planned for 2027. Only then could Heathrow expansion be aligned with the net-zero goal, adds the committee. As part of this new strategy, the CCC says the “aviation sector needs to take responsibility for its emissions”. It says policies should be designed based on the “polluter pays” principle, req
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/ccc-heathrow-expansion-could-push-flights-to-80-of-uk-emissions-by-2050

* In Dubai, Trade Continues on the Front Lines of the Iran War
  New Lines | 15 Sep 10:44 UTC
  After more than six months of war with Iran, the UAE has vowed to stop all trade with its neighbor. Yet fruit and vegetables from Iran still reach the Gulf states daily, showing how tightly the region’s economies are bound together. The post In Dubai, Trade Continues on the Front Lines of the Iran War appeared first on New Lines Magazine ...
  FULL TEXT: Rashed Aljabri swings a watermelon from the truck to his colleague standing on the floor of Al Aweer, Dubai’s largest fruit and vegetable market. After building up a pyramid-like stack, he slices one in half with a knife and cuts out a scarlet segment from the center. “Iran, Iran, Iran,” Aljabri chants. It is 6 a.m., and pallet trucks loaded with cauliflowers, boxes of tomatoes, bulbous pumpkins, eggplants and broccoli in ice-filled foam boxes whiz around the wholesale market, narrowly avoiding beeping tuk-tuks in near-orchestrated harmony. “Ninety percent of the produce in this market is from Iran,” Aljabri estimates. Containers marked with Tehran addresses reverse into the docking bays, traders unloading trolleys of salad and celery. More than six months into the war with Iran, with no end in sight, the United Arab Emirates is once again ramping up pressure on its neighbor across the Gulf, announcing last month that it would halt all trade ties. The move, announced in response to Iranian strikes on Emirati ships, is seen as part of the Trump administration’s strategy of economically strangling Iran. But Iranian watermelons, and much more, are still flowing into Dubai’s markets, feeding its restaurants, supermarkets and hotels, and providing one small example of how difficult it would be to fully separate the two countries’ historically linked economies. Although the Strait of Hormuz has today become a geopolitical choke point, blocking the flow of oil, fertilizer and global trade, the area has long been a local hub of economic and cultural exchange. For centuries, merchants from Iran and the Arabian Peninsula sailed across the Gulf on wooden dhows, ferrying saffron, pearls, spices and pottery. Over the years, communities in the ports of Dubai, Bandar Abbas and Bandar Lengeh became a melting pot of Persian and Arabic culture, speaking both languages. Despite the ongoing conflict, this ancient trade is still visible in Dubai. In the old town, impressively large turquoise and white dhows parade the port at Marfa Wharf, cranes loading their cargo, soon ready to sail. In 1979, the Iranian Revolution sparked a huge influx of wealthy Iranian business owners and educated professionals fleeing the new Islamic Republic. Today the UAE is home to some 560,000 Iranians, the vast majority in Dubai. They own businesses, buy property and employ workers, representing a cornerstone of the economy. * Where in the world is the iPhone cheapest?
  Rest of World | 15 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Apple’s high-end models cost more in markets like India and Turkey owing to high taxes and import duties.
  FULL TEXT: The buzz around Apple’s new phone launches is global, but the best deals haven’t made their way outside the U.S. The iPhone 18 Pro and 18 Pro Max will cost more than they do in the U.S. in all other major iPhone markets, according to market intelligence firm Counterpoint Research. The two models will cost the highest in Turkey, nearly double the U.S. price, due to local taxes, Counterpoint says. In India — iPhone’s fourth-largest market and the second-largest manufacturing hub for Apple — prices will be 30% to 40% higher than in the U.S. for similar reasons. The steep price tags persist despite Apple’s continued efforts to diversify manufacturing operations to reduce dependence on China. India now accounts for about 25% of Apple’s total global iPhone production, but the new foldable model Duo is not being produced in the country. “India pricing is steep, and the difference is primarily due to the fact that initial Duo sales will not be ‘Made in India’ for some time,” Tarun Pathak, director of devices and ecosystems at Counterpoint Research, told Rest of World . “Consequently, imported units attract full import duty — making them significantly more expensive — along with GST [goods and services tax] and the impact of a weaker rupee.” The U.S., as always, continues to have the most affordable prices in the world for the latest launches. The second-cheapest market, the UAE, has a relatively smaller price tag not just for the iPhone but also for other brands and models, due to a low value-added tax at 5% and no other taxes. 25% of Apple’s global iPhone production is now from India. In China and Korea, prices skew lower not only due to different tax rates but also because of robust local market competition. “China is highly competitive with Huawei, Oppo, Vivo, while Samsung is the heavyweight in Korea,” Harshit Rastogi, senior analyst at Counterpoint Research, told Rest of World . Even the made-in-India iPhones are still largely only assembled in the country. Only 18% to 20% of electronic components originate domestically, while the rest are imported and taxed heavily. “The Pro models have even higher import dependence than the base model,” Rastogi said. “As a greater share of components becomes locally sourced in the coming years, price increases are likely to become more gradual.” In June, Apple’s former CEO Tim Cook warned that the price of existing models would go up due to a shortage of chips.   Homecomings, fantasies, food and ghosts – how the myth of eternal return keeps the Italian diaspora in a state of longing - by Ben Faccini Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: All original watercolours by Emily Faccini When he was a child growing up in Emilia-Romagna, my great-grandfather Marco once found an eagle fledgling that had fallen from its nest. He kept it warm in the folds of his shirt and ran home, only to discover as he undressed that the bird had eaten strips of his flesh, its beak tinged red with blood. The rescued eagle thrived in my great-grandfather’s care, its wingspan soon a remarkable length. And all the while, Marco himself grew immensely tall. Then, one day, without warning, the eagle flew away. The moment my father told me this story when I was a boy, it lodged itself deep in my imagination. I pictured young Marco as a giant striding through the forests of north-central Italy, calling to the bird of prey, waiting in vain for it to land on his outstretched hand. Marco merged with the heroes of my illustrated classical civilisation books, earning his place between the busts of Roman emperors and the chapters on Greek mythology. What I never realised was that this family legend held clues to who I am and to my relationship to Italy. L ina Stores deli in Soho, London, has been a pillar of Italian life in England for decades. It’s where we’d sometimes meet our paternal grandparents when visiting London from our home in France. My brothers and I would line up in front of the refrigerated counter and ask for our favourite ravioli, doing our utmost to avoid any slip-ups in Italian that would make the staff wince: ‘ ricotta e spinaci ’, ‘ zucca ’, ‘ funghi porcini ’. Back at our grandparents’ flat, Nonna served the ravioli on an elaborately laid table, and my grandfather appeared with freshly grated Parmigiano. He’d sprinkle the cheese evenly over our pasta; never too much, nor too little. How he did this, and with such speed, we didn’t know. If we had spaghetti, Nonna would wrap her right hand around ours and twirl the fork with us so we held just the right amount, the bespattered tablecloth damning proof of our slapdash habits at home. My grandparents trusted that these rituals of the table (they saw them as basic codes of Italianness, connecting displaced Italians everywhere) would allow us to paper over the differences in our ethnically blended and peripatetic family. In this way, we’d be able to claim Italian identity for ourselves, despite its competition in our lives with Englishness and Frenchness.   FULL TEXT: I am at the airport gate waiting to depart, watching passengers hurry past with rolling suitcases and paper cups of coffee. A small boy sits very still beside his mother. He holds an iPad close to his chest, almost in a cramp, clutching the screen as if it might otherwise run away. I hear loud clapping hands and squeaking children’s voices. I lean over and see a cartoon character in green snatch a toy from another character in pink. The pink character begins to cry, tears splashing from enormous eyes like sprinkles. The green character shouts sorry. Instantly, the tears stop and a wide smile replaces the tears before I have blinked. And, before I have even worked out whether the characters are speaking English or Arabic (as the mother does to the boy), the scene has changed and the two characters are rowing a boat with a random song in the background. The boy’s eyes dart on the screen from right to left, centre, down. His body is still but his attention is being hurried from cue to cue. This is the problem I have come to think of as the pace of children’s media. Children’s media are usually judged by their content and quantity: how many minutes watched and whether it was educational, age-appropriate, diverse, prosocial, commercial or safe. These questions matter and, with my colleagues, I have studied them through experiments and contributed to guidelines, regulations, or toolkits for designers. But we have been missing a bigger design feature that may be just as consequential: the time a medium gives a child to make meaning. A programme or app can contain good lessons and still move too quickly for those lessons to settle. For instance, in the US animated educational series Ask the StoryBots (2016-20), the subject matter may be genuinely rich – why the sky is blue, how eyes work, or where planets come from – but the explanation unfolds through rapid jokes, songs, visual shifts and sudden changes in style. Before a child has time to sit with one image or idea, the scene has moved on to the next, with a burst of information. Different media have different temporal demands: a digital picture book, a game, a television episode and an app each ask a child to attend differently. But, across media, the important question is whether the pace suits the experience that the narrative claims to support.   https://aeon.co/essays/its-not-just-screen-time-kids-are-bombarded-by-hectic-media

  FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Arun Sankar/AFP via Getty Images) In a Trumpian world, shock value is a depreciating currency, even for the man at its center. This week, however, U.S. President Donald Trump managed to spend some anyway, pledging a $5,000 “Trump dividend” to every adult American citizen if Republicans retain control of Congress come November. The news expectedly drew outrage, with critics calling the stunt a “bribe” and pointing to the president’s earlier unfulfilled promises of similar dividends, but across large swaths of the developing democratic world, it landed less as a scandal than a recognition of a long-entrenched practice. The line between a state benefit and vote-buying payouts shouldn’t be blurry, but in countries such as India, the Philippines, Kenya, Egypt and Indonesia, the practice is so widespread that voters no longer find flaw in politicians handing out cash or staples like rice, oil and sugar during election cycles, or after. In fact, Indian politicians have graduated from cash to a full dowry — cows, goats, laptops, bicycles, blenders, wristwatches, even gold, drugs and alcohol — as if courting the electorate required a trousseau. The 2019 national election saw the country’s electoral commission seize cash and goods worth roughly $475 million distributed to buy votes; by the 2024 election, that figure had more than doubled to over $1 billion. In Kenya, too, cash and in-kind gifts have been distributed openly at election rallies, while in Egypt, party workers have reportedly paid people roughly up to $5 outside polling sites, often topping it with a box of groceries and tickets to a Cairo amusement park. Indonesians have called this “politik uang,” or “money politics.” Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi coined a term of his own — “revdi culture” — using the name of the north Indian sweet as a stand-in for “freebies.” American political language has no equivalent shorthand for any of this yet. Even as Modi has attempted to shame both voters and political parties for this indulgence, his own Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) hasn’t been able to resist the temptation. With a government payment system in place, India’s version of payouts lately comes disguised as public cash transfer schemes targeted particularly at women, promised on the campaign trail in the hope of winning votes, and rolled out once the party assumes power. * The Language of Terrorism Since 9/11
  New Lines | 11 Sep 10:51 UTC
  While the world’s media have retreated from using the word "terrorism," governments have applied it ever more broadly, with vast implications for law and society. How does this simultaneous avoidance and expansion of the use of the word affect how we understand and address the phenomenon of violence committed in the name of an ideal? The ...
  FULL TEXT: The 20th century saw vast developments in the capacity of nations to kill, maim and control. But perhaps the single biggest shift in waging war was that from fighting on the ground to taking to the skies. Suddenly, weapons — whether ballistic or chemical — could come from above, tapping into ancient archetypes of fear. Violent death could come at any time, in any place, with minimal warning, fundamentally changing the theater of conflict. Anywhere could be — and was — targeted. Blitzes razed whole cities with no concern for who was there. Eventually, the technology brought nuclear weapons to the cities of Hiroshima and Nagasaki, which seemed to be the pinnacle of human destruction. But a hallmark of humans is to continually adapt, finding new ways to live — and to kill. Although the use of nuclear weapons has been constrained by human horror at the effects and by subsequent international treaties, there has been a proliferation of other threats from the skies. Drones also strike without warning, at a fraction of the financial cost of other airborne weapons; they also spy on everyday activity, inspiring paranoia and yet more fear. And, 25 years ago, passenger planes were used to inflict the largest one-day death toll inflicted by an enemy of the United States on American soil, with effects, both physical and psychological, that continue to this day. The 9/11 attacks soon came to symbolize terrorism, with images of planes penetrating the twin towers in downtown New York used to illustrate and convey the feeling of terror coming from the skies. But news organizations have moved away from using the word “terrorism” to describe the events of that day 25 years ago. From Reuters to the BBC, official guidelines state that best practice is to use descriptions of specific forms of violence (hijackings, bombings, knife attacks) rather than the label “terrorism,” arguing that the term itself is value-laden whereas the actions themselves are not. The ultimate aim of bearing witness through journalism, the guidelines explain, is best served by neutral language that does not include the judgment inherent in the word “terrorist.” Yet while the world’s media have retreated from the use of such language, governments have applied it ever more broadly, with vast implications for law and society. Terrorism legislation, especially that devised within the framework of the war on terror, uses definitions of terrorism vague enough to be expanded, seemingly indefinitely. From antif
  https://newlinesmag.com/essays/the-language-of-terrorism-since-9-11/

In the U.S., frontier AI labs have mostly competed for the best researchers and engineers. In Asia, however, the more sought-after executives are those who can translate AI capabilities into market adoption as the competition moves from building the best models to capturing new markets. In new markets, companies need leaders who can focus on partnerships, policy, and business development, Deepali Vyas, global head of data and AI executive search at talent advisory firm ZRG Partners, told Rest of World . Vyas works with Fortune 500 companies to scout talent for executive roles. “Once a company decides [a region] is a strategic growth market, you start looking for executives who can essentially operate as mini-CEOs,” she said. A professional with experience working at companies like Microsoft, Google, or Uber in Asia has an understanding of how Silicon Valley’s products can be scaled while navigating local landscapes, making them valuable to AI companies. Experts believe AI companies’ focus is less on poaching from big tech firms and more on seeking individuals who have such experience. * Penchants of the polymaths
  Aeon | 11 Sep 10:00 UTC
  The fathers of Islamic science hold lessons for students: breadth over specialisation, and never let constraints get in the way - by Mariam Sabri Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: From Chronology of Ancient Nations ( c 1000 CE) by Abu Rayhan al-Biruni. Courtesy Edinburgh University , Scotland This semester, my students are learning about four polymaths who shaped Islamic science: al-Biruni, Alhazen, Avicenna and al-Khwarizmi. Their circumstances could hardly have been more different: one wrote his way out of scarcity; one was held under house arrest for a decade; one distilled an era’s medical knowledge into a text that ruled European universities for 500 years; and the last worked at the largest research institution of his age. But each shared the same underlying instinct: to move freely between mathematics, medicine, astronomy, philosophy and poetry, testing one discipline’s assumptions against another’s. My classroom is in Pakistan, where students arrive eager to talk about disruption and employability, but rarely about breadth. I want them to see, through these four lives, that this kind of expansiveness isn’t a luxury for stable times; it has produced some of the most enduring breakthroughs in the history of science. After 15 years working in liberal arts education, I believe a reintegration of disciplines in a Hellenistic style can lead the way. I’m not making a superficial case for ‘interdisciplinarity’. Instead, I’m arguing for something more specific: the recovery of the penchants of polymathy that once thrived in the Muslim world. Rather than fetishising disruptive innovation through compulsory courses on entrepreneurship and applied methods, we should offer an entirely different model of how to learn and what learning is for – especially since my students already enter university with a narrowly vocational mindset. The word polymathy itself captures the bricolage of many ( poly ) worlds of knowledge ( mathy ). Polymathy offers respite and reflection in a world fractured by war, climate catastrophe and injustice. It is sourced from indigenous idioms of the Muslim world rather than an imposition by the Global North. Today, polymathy brings different kinds of thinking to bear on political, economic and environmental problems. At a time when students are reading less of the classic canon and worrying more about employment opportunities, the expansiveness of polymathy can offer a meaningful path forward. These are the lessons of the great Islamic polymaths. T he life of our opening actor al-Biruni has much to teach students about the human dimensions of science.   FULL TEXT: In May 1992, I went on a real estate tour of suburban West Bank settlements. The tour was part of the government-sponsored “Operation Populate Judea and Samaria,” which referred to the biblical name of the territory known by most of the world as the West Bank. Most of the passengers on our bus were young couples trying to figure out how to afford a first home. In one settlement, they were offered 680-square-foot homes, with the legal right to expand their houses as their families grew, for a fraction of the price of a similar-sized city flat. An official told us, while we stood outside another settlement, that we could obtain a government mortgage for 95% of the house’s value, and that some of its value could be turned into a grant. In short, it was a giveaway. A crucial election was a month away. The ruling right-wing Likud Party, led by Yitzhak Shamir, feared that Yitzhak Rabin’s Labor Party would win, and would be willing to trade pieces of the occupied West Bank for peace with the Palestinians. Once Israeli citizens bought homes, though, it would be hard to keep them from moving in. Once they moved in, the political price of forcing them to leave would be too high to pay. “What if Rabin wins? What if they give it back?” a young man with a visibly pregnant wife asked our guide. “We don’t build here to give it back,” the guide answered. Rabin did win. He went on to sign the Oslo Accords, a supposedly interim agreement that turned over part of the West Bank to limited Palestinian rule — and which left all the settlements and much land between them under full Israeli control, their fate awaiting a final peace agreement that has yet to come. The “interim” arrangement has lasted ever since. Settlement building achieved its goal, which was to leave future Israeli governments fewer options for peacemaking. Today, Israel has a right-wing government more extreme and less inhibited than Shamir’s. Given current polling numbers, it has even more reason to be fearful about an election two months away. This fear is driving a frenzy of settlement activity in the West Bank. Google Earth has long been a trusted infrastructure for the verification field, and is the platform on which open-source investigators are formally trained for conflict work in Syria, Yemen, and Sudan. Yet on July 30, Google launched a new feature that built image generation directly into Google Earth , the product that for two decades was synonymous with satellite image truth. New ways to fabricate launch overnight, a storm of criticism ensues, and guardrails arrive only after the harm.” It meant that users could zoom in to real coordinates, type a prompt, and get an AI-generated photorealistic scene blended into actual satellite and aerial imagery. Within hours, users had conjured a nuclear plant in Iran , a bomb crater beside a Gaza hospital , and floods lapping the U.S. Capitol. Faking satellite imagery is not new. Google’s own watermarks had exposed its Gemini AI model being used to fake satellite evidence, a recurring feature of Iran’s state-affiliated propaganda. But the Google Earth feature provided greater ease and accessibility inside the very tool that was known for trusted information. Within a day, following an outcry from investigative experts and the media, Google pulled the feature, saying it would return with “stronger guardrails.” That is our information environment in a nutshell: New ways to fabricate launch overnight, a storm of criticism ensues, and guardrails arrive only after the harm. Meanwhile, the U.S. government ordered satellite companies to restrict journalists’ access to imagery in the region, citing wartime security, narrowing the supply of real information even as the counterfeits spread. This was not the first generative AI product from a big tech company to be pulled or shelved recently. OpenAI shut down its Sora video app in March following sustained public demands for its withdrawal.   FULL TEXT: All images from the Codex Manesse (1300-40) and courtesy Heidelberg University Library Early one morning in the late 12th century, Bishop Hugh of Lincoln arrived in the Hertfordshire village of Cheshunt. There, he was immediately surrounded by a crowd of people, who begged him to help one of their neighbours. And so, dismounting from his horse, he went to the man’s house, where he was confronted by the disturbing sight of a possessed man ‘lying prostrate with his head tied to a post and each of his hands to great stakes fixed in the ground.’ Despite these restraints, the man constantly writhed around, rolling his eyes, gnashing his teeth, and sticking his tongue out. To Hugh, as to the local onlookers, the diagnosis was clear: this unfortunate sailor had been possessed by a demon and could be cured only by an exorcism. Once the necessary rituals were performed, he was ‘delivered from the demon [and] … after living devoutly for some years, finally made a good and peaceful end.’ For Hugh’s biographer, Adam of Eynsham, the incident was worth recounting because it demonstrated not only that the future saint had healing powers, but that he was a brave and compassionate man (in sharp contrast to the local bishop, who had ‘galloped away as if pursued by the Furies’, rather than face a possessed man). But, to us, the story – full of fear, cruelty and superstition – sums up both everything that was wrong with medieval attitudes to mental illness, and the apparent uselessness of medieval medicine in the face of serious illness. A nd yet, as is so often the case when studying the distant past, the reality was rather more complicated. Influenced by stories of demonic possession (and there are lots of them, especially in saints’ lives and miracle collections), I long assumed that our ancestors’ attitudes to, and experiences of, mental health were completely different to our own – and that they would have little sympathy with our fast-growing mental health crisis. But when I turned my attention away from religious texts, and began to research medieval ideas about healthy living, I soon came across medieval people who discussed mental health in ways that felt far more familiar to me. The similarities were particularly apparent in medical writings, which outline a sophisticated argument that mental wellness was at the heart of a healthy lifestyle.   FULL TEXT: What do privilege and accountability look like for an AI agent on the wrong side of the law? Evan Ratliff rounds up a collection of challenging cases in which AI agents have functioned as co-conspirators in criminal acts, offered courtroom statements on behalf of deceased victims, and perpetrated deeds that, for humans, are considered to be illegal. Legal teams have already sought to seal their clients’ chats with AI agents, likening the exchanges to conversations with an attorney. Ratliff’s feature is an omnibus, and you’ve likely encountered one or two of his stories via other outlets. Here, the concerns he gathers together reveal the breadth of challenges facing the American legal system. AI chatbots are already serving as confidants and co-conspirators, assisting and even encouraging humans acting on their darkest impulses. They’re occupying ersatz roles as therapists and lawyers, relationships our legal system typically shields with confidential privilege. They’re producing new kinds of criminal evidence and offering up the beguiling possibility of preventing crime before it happens. And as they’re given more autonomy across our digital and physical systems, they’re even gaining the ability to commit crimes themselves, absent specific human instruction — and, perhaps, in defiance of it. “AI companion toys for neurodivergent children promise connection and therapy, but can they deliver for the long haul?” “Jesse Genet’s time was scarce.   FULL TEXT: Under the warm rays of the coastal sun in Syria’s Tartus region, Ghazal Imad Rustum, 20, marched, an olive-green cap crowning her head. Her words were laced with the tinge of a Beiruti accent. “But the experience has been nothing short of incredible.” In late 2025, Syria’s Ministry of Interior released a recruitment notice for women. Rustum saw a link on social media and decided to apply to “serve her country.” Initially, her family was surprised at her decision, but soon began to encourage and support her. The scope of the role the young women were being recruited for was unclear, but the notice still prompted the excited enrollment of about 15,000 from all over the country. Last year, I visited the Ministry of Interior to ask for details. “We’ve had so many women come to us lately, and we’ve had to redirect them,” said the woman conducting searches at the ministry’s entrance. One woman I spoke to at the time was worried her height wouldn’t meet the required threshold of 160 centimeters. A month or so later, I saw Facebook posts about applicants beginning to receive their acceptances. The comments were filled with women anxiously asking questions, as others (also mostly women) attempted to answer them: “Hi, I’ve sent in my application, but haven’t received a reply yet.” “Can we only enroll from specific governorates?” “Is the height requirement mandatory?” There are two police institutes in Syria that train women police. One of them is located in Damascus’ al-Tall, the other on a Tartus highway, with “Training Branch of Tartus” emblazoned in gold against Syria’s official, dark green. When I visited the Tartus site in March, the women were being trained as tourism police. Their daily routine consisted of exercise in the morning, rest time, lectures, then weapons training. In the first month of the four-month training, the women stayed at the institute throughout. After that, they visited their families every weekend. * Making revisions
  Aeon | 09 Sep 10:01 UTC
  Indigenous knowledge and art overwrite colonial cartography to correct the historical record in Central Australia - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A common narrative among European settlers was that the lands they occupied were empty and, therefore, free to claim. This assumption, imposed across many colonies, severed Indigenous peoples from places to which they held deep cultural and spiritual ties. Central Australia was no exception: the doctrine of ‘terra nullius’, used to justify British claims over Australia, treated the land as if it belonged to no one, disregarding the long histories of Aboriginal communities who had been living there for tens of thousands of years. For this short documentary, the British artist Patrick Waterhouse collaborated with the Warlukurlangu art centre to revisit that history by intervening in official colonial documents and maps. Working for several years with Warlpiri artists from Yuendumu and Nyirripi, he invited them to respond to these archival materials through their own visual languages. The film captures the collaborative process, the conversations prompted by these documents and the striking works that emerge: pieces in which colonial cartography intersects with Indigenous knowledge and art. * Now it’s China’s experts who are gig workers training AI
  Rest of World | 09 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Squeezed by a stagnant economy and state directives, China's underemployed lawyers, architects, and engineers are taking cheap gig work to teach AI their exact trades — just to pay this month's mortgage.
  FULL TEXT: As a Shenzhen-based architect in her 40s, Cuicui has vast experience designing public stadiums, schools, and hospitals at an architecture firm. But over the past five years, declining government infrastructure spending caused a sharp drop in business. Her income halved. To keep paying her mortgage and cover her child’s extracurricular fees, Cuicui became a specialized AI trainer. At the end of her regular workday, she would log on to a data annotation platform called TalentsAI, and give an AI model step-by-step instructions on how to write up a building proposal or run profitability analyses. “It is just like teaching a child,” Cuicui told Rest of World . “Every task has to come from my actual work.” You have to keep evolving yourself to avoid getting completely replaced.” A Shanghai-based software engineer with two decades of experience who began taking AI training gigs in July. In the U.S., data companies like Mercor, Surge AI, and Handshake have already recruited tens of thousands of Hollywood filmmakers , lawyers , and scientists to train AI models on various white-collar tasks. These jobs are now emerging in China, as the government pushes for AI adoption across the economy. In June, China’s National Data Administration threw its support behind building “high-quality data sets,” and called for more industry experts to be brought deeper into data annotation to raise the “knowledge density” of AI training data. It encouraged college graduates to take up annotation work as a source of flexible employment. Rest of World spoke with a cohort of expert-level AI trainers who, like Cuicui, requested anonymity to avoid being identified by employers, colleagues, or the training platforms they feed. They said the work is precarious, demanding, and lower-paid than in America. But as they face a tough job market and disruption from AI, the Chinese professionals say they have taken the gigs to make money as well as to participate in what they believe to be an industry of the future.
